<a id='question-048'></a>

### Question 048: RBC Pathology and Anemias: Focus 01-48 (weakness, claudication, fatigue, and lethargy.)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology and molecular basis of hereditary spherocytosis, differentiating it from other hemolytic anemias based on specific laboratory findings and peripheral blood smear morphology. (理解遗传性球形红细胞增多症的病理生理学和分子基础，根据特定的实验室检查结果和外周血涂片形态将其与其他溶血性贫血区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy presents to the pediatrician with a history of recurrent episodes of jaundice, fatigue, and dark urine, particularly after viral infections. His mother reports that he tires easily during play and has difficulty keeping up with his peers. Physical examination reveals mild scleral icterus and splenomegaly. Laboratory studies show a hemoglobin level of 9.5 g/dL (normal range: 11.5-15.5 g/dL), MCV of 80 fL (normal range: 77-95 fL), MCH of 27 pg (normal range: 27-33 pg), RDW of 15% (normal range: 11.5-14.5%), reticulocyte count of 5% (normal range: 0.5-2.5%), elevated indirect bilirubin, and a negative direct antiglobulin test (DAT, Coombs test). A peripheral blood smear shows numerous small, round, densely stained red blood cells lacking central pallor, consistent with spherocytes. Family history is significant for similar symptoms in his maternal grandfather.

(A) Deficiency in the enzyme glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) leading to oxidative stress-induced hemolysis.
(B) Autoimmune destruction of red blood cells mediated by IgG antibodies against Rh antigens.
(C) Defect in the red blood cell membrane proteins (ankyrin, spectrin, band 3, or protein 4.2) leading to loss of membrane surface area and spherocyte formation.
(D) Impaired synthesis of heme due to a mutation in the ALAS2 gene, resulting in microcytic hypochromic anemia and basophilic stippling.
(E) Increased red blood cell destruction due to complement activation triggered by cold agglutinins (IgM antibodies) against the I antigen.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 6-year-old boy with recurrent jaundice, fatigue, dark urine, and easy tiring, especially after infections. This suggests a chronic hemolytic anemia exacerbated by stress.
*   **Physical Exam:** Scleral icterus (jaundice) and splenomegaly (extramedullary hematopoiesis and RBC sequestration/destruction).
*   **Labs:**
    *   Low Hemoglobin (9.5 g/dL): Anemia.
    *   Normal MCV (80 fL) and MCH (27 pg): Normocytic, normochromic anemia (though MCH is borderline low).
    *   High RDW (15%): Increased variation in RBC size, common in hemolytic anemias and iron deficiency.
    *   High Reticulocyte Count (5%): Bone marrow compensation for hemolysis.
    *   Elevated Indirect Bilirubin: Product of heme breakdown from hemolysis.
    *   Negative DAT (Coombs test): Rules out autoimmune hemolytic anemia.
*   **Peripheral Blood Smear:** Spherocytes (small, round, dense RBCs without central pallor). This is a key morphological finding.
*   **Family History:** Similar symptoms in maternal grandfather, suggesting an inherited disorder.
*   **Synthesis:** The combination of chronic hemolytic anemia (jaundice, fatigue, dark urine, splenomegaly, high reticulocytes, high indirect bilirubin), negative DAT, characteristic spherocytes on smear, and positive family history strongly points towards Hereditary Spherocytosis (HS).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Hereditary Spherocytosis (HS):** This is an inherited disorder of the red blood cell membrane. It is typically autosomal dominant (though recessive forms exist).
*   **Mechanism:** Defects in proteins that link the RBC membrane skeleton (spectrin, ankyrin, band 3, protein 4.2) to the lipid bilayer lead to a loss of membrane surface area relative to cell volume. This causes the RBCs to become spherical (spherocytes) instead of biconcave discs.
*   **Consequences:** Spherocytes are less deformable than normal RBCs. They are trapped and prematurely destroyed in the spleen (extravascular hemolysis), leading to anemia, jaundice, and splenomegaly. The negative DAT distinguishes HS from autoimmune hemolytic anemia.
*   **Molecular Basis:** Mutations in genes encoding ankyrin (ANK1), band 3 (SLC4A1), spectrin alpha (SPTA1), spectrin beta (SPTB), or protein 4.2 (EPB42) are responsible. Ankyrin deficiency is the most common cause.
*   **Clinical Features:** Varying degrees of anemia, jaundice, splenomegaly, and gallstones (due to chronic bilirubin overload). Episodes of aplastic crisis (triggered by parvovirus B19) or hemolytic crisis (triggered by infections) can occur.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Deficiency in the enzyme glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD) leading to oxidative stress-induced hemolysis:** G6PD deficiency is an X-linked inherited disorder causing episodic hemolytic anemia triggered by oxidative stress (e.g., certain drugs, infections, fava beans). While it causes hemolysis, the peripheral smear typically shows bite cells and Heinz bodies (though spherocytes can sometimes be seen), not predominantly spherocytes. The DAT is negative. This is a plausible differential for episodic hemolysis but doesn't fit the chronic nature and predominant spherocytosis seen here.
*   **(B) Autoimmune destruction of red blood cells mediated by IgG antibodies against Rh antigens:** This describes autoimmune hemolytic anemia (AIHA), specifically warm AIHA. AIHA is characterized by a positive DAT (Coombs test), which is explicitly negative in this patient. The morphology on smear is typically variable but often includes spherocytes, but the positive DAT is the defining feature absent here.
*   **(D) Impaired synthesis of heme due to a mutation in the ALAS2 gene, resulting in microcytic hypochromic anemia and basophilic stippling:** This describes X-linked sideroblastic anemia (XLSA), caused by mutations in the ALAS2 gene encoding aminolevulinate synthase 2. It leads to impaired heme synthesis, resulting in microcytic, hypochromic anemia with ring sideroblasts in the bone marrow and basophilic stippling on the peripheral smear. This patient has normocytic anemia and spherocytes, not microcytic hypochromic anemia with basophilic stippling.
*   **(E) Increased red blood cell destruction due to complement activation triggered by cold agglutinins (IgM antibodies) against the I antigen:** This describes cold agglutinin disease (CAD), a type of autoimmune hemolytic anemia. It is characterized by hemolysis triggered by cold temperatures, often associated with IgM antibodies (cold agglutinins) against the I antigen. The DAT is typically positive for C3d only. While hemolysis occurs, the characteristic finding is agglutination on the smear in cold conditions, and the DAT is usually positive (though sometimes only for complement), unlike this case.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Hereditary Spherocytosis (HS) | G6PD Deficiency | Autoimmune Hemolytic Anemia (AIHA) | X-linked Sideroblastic Anemia (XLSA) | Cold Agglutinin Disease (CAD) |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------- | :-------------- | :--------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :---------------------------- |
| **Inheritance**         | AD (most common)              | X-linked        | Acquired (usually)                 | X-linked                             | Acquired (usually)            |
| **Mechanism**           | RBC membrane defect           | Oxidative stress| Autoantibodies (IgG/IgM)          | Impaired heme synthesis              | Cold agglutinins (IgM)        |
| **DAT (Coombs)**        | Negative                      | Negative        | Positive (IgG or C3d)              | Negative                             | Positive (C3d often)          |
| **Smear Morphology**    | Spherocytes                   | Bite cells, Heinz bodies | Spherocytes, schistocytes (variable) | Microcytic, hypochromic, basophilic stippling, ring sideroblasts (BM) | Agglutination (cold)         |
| **MCV**                 | Normal/Slightly Low           | Normal          | Normal/Variable                    | Low                                  | Normal/Variable               |
| **Reticulocytes**       | High                          | High (during crisis) | High                               | Normal/Low                          | High                          |
| **Bilirubin (Indirect)** | High                          | High (during crisis) | High                               | Normal                               | High                          |
| **Splenomegaly**        | Common                        | No              | Variable                           | No                                   | Variable                      |
| **Trigger**             | Infections                    | Drugs, infection, fava beans | Variable                           | None specific                       | Cold exposure                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hereditary spherocytosis is characterized by inherited RBC membrane defects leading to spherocyte formation, extravascular hemolysis, negative DAT, and splenomegaly. Key differentials include G6PD deficiency (episodic, oxidative stress trigger, bite cells), AIHA (positive DAT), and other causes of hemolytic anemia. Remember the classic triad: spherocytes, negative DAT, and family history.

<a id='question-049'></a>

### Question 049: RBC Pathology and Anemias: Focus 01-49 (• Reticulocytes are immature, larger red cells (ma)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology of hereditary spherocytosis, specifically the role of membrane protein defects (e.g., spectrin, ankyrin) leading to decreased red blood cell surface area-to-volume ratio, increased osmotic fragility, and characteristic spherocyte morphology. (理解遗传性球形红细胞增多症的病理生理学，特别是膜蛋白缺陷（如谱蛋白、锚蛋白）导致红细胞表面积/体积比降低、渗透脆性增加和球形红细胞形态的特征。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 65-year-old African American woman with a history of compensated hemolytic anemia presents to her primary care physician complaining of worsening fatigue and jaundice over the past month. She denies fever, chills, or recent infections. Her past medical history is significant for hypertension, well-controlled with lisinopril. Physical examination reveals mild scleral icterus and splenomegaly. Vital signs are stable. Laboratory studies show: Hemoglobin 9.5 g/dL (Normal: 12-16 g/dL), MCV 80 fL (Normal: 80-100 fL), MCH 27 pg (Normal: 27-33 pg), MCHC 34 g/dL (Normal: 32-36 g/dL), Reticulocyte count 5% (Normal: 0.5-2.5%), Total bilirubin 2.5 mg/dL (Normal: 0.3-1.2 mg/dL), Indirect bilirubin 1.8 mg/dL (Normal: 0.3-0.8 mg/dL), LDH 450 U/L (Normal: 100-220 U/L), Haptoglobin <10 mg/dL (Normal: 30-200 mg/dL), Direct Antiglobulin Test (DAT) negative. Peripheral blood smear shows numerous small, round, densely staining red blood cells lacking central pallor, consistent with spherocytes. Osmotic fragility testing reveals increased fragility.

What is the most likely underlying molecular defect responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Deficiency in the enzyme glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD), leading to oxidative stress-induced hemolysis.
(B) Defect in the synthesis of the beta-globin chain, resulting in unstable hemoglobin and premature red cell destruction.
(C) Mutation in the gene encoding ankyrin, causing impaired linkage of the red blood cell membrane skeleton to the lipid bilayer.
(D) Autoimmune destruction of red blood cells mediated by IgG antibodies against Rh antigens.
(E) Impaired synthesis of heme due to a defect in ferrochelatase, leading to accumulation of protoporphyrin IX in red blood cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 65-year-old African American woman. Hereditary spherocytosis (HS) is common in Northern European descent but can occur in other populations. Family history is not mentioned, but it's a hereditary condition.
*   **Symptoms:** Worsening fatigue and jaundice suggest increased hemolysis.
*   **Physical Exam:** Scleral icterus (jaundice) and splenomegaly are classic signs of chronic hemolysis, where the spleen removes abnormal red cells.
*   **Labs:**
    *   Low Hemoglobin (9.5 g/dL): Anemia.
    *   Normal MCV (80 fL), MCH (27 pg), MCHC (34 g/dL): Normocytic, normochromic anemia, but the MCHC is at the upper limit of normal or slightly elevated, which is characteristic of spherocytes (increased concentration of hemoglobin within a smaller volume).
    *   High Reticulocyte count (5%): Compensatory response to hemolysis.
    *   Elevated Total and Indirect Bilirubin: Due to increased breakdown of heme from destroyed red cells.
    *   Elevated LDH: Released from damaged red cells.
    *   Low Haptoglobin: Binds free hemoglobin released during hemolysis; consumed in intravascular hemolysis, but can also be low in extravascular hemolysis like HS.
    *   Negative DAT: Rules out autoimmune hemolytic anemia.
*   **Peripheral Smear:** Spherocytes (small, round, dense RBCs without central pallor) are the hallmark morphological finding.
*   **Osmotic Fragility:** Increased fragility confirms the structural defect making the cells less deformable and more susceptible to lysis in hypotonic solutions.
*   **Diagnosis:** The constellation of chronic hemolytic anemia, jaundice, splenomegaly, spherocytes on smear, increased osmotic fragility, and negative DAT strongly points towards Hereditary Spherocytosis (HS).

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
*   **Hereditary Spherocytosis Pathophysiology:** HS is caused by defects in proteins that form the red blood cell membrane skeleton, which anchors the lipid bilayer to the underlying cytoskeleton. The most common defects involve spectrin (alpha or beta chains), ankyrin, band 3 (anion exchanger 1), or protein 4.2. These defects lead to a loss of membrane surface area relative to cell volume. The cell attempts to maintain a spherical shape, resulting in the characteristic spherocyte morphology.
*   **Consequences:** Spherocytes are less deformable than normal biconcave discs. This reduced deformability makes them prone to entrapment and destruction in the splenic cords (sinusoids), leading to extravascular hemolysis, anemia, jaundice, and splenomegaly.
*   **Molecular Basis:** Mutations in genes encoding these membrane proteins disrupt their structure, function, or interaction with other proteins. For example, mutations in the *ANK1* gene (encoding ankyrin) are a common cause, impairing the linkage between the membrane skeleton (spectrin) and the lipid bilayer (via band 3).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **G6PD Deficiency:** This is an enzyme defect causing episodic hemolysis triggered by oxidative stress (e.g., drugs, infections, fava beans). While it causes hemolytic anemia, jaundice, and elevated LDH/bilirubin, the peripheral smear typically shows bite cells and Heinz bodies (with special stains), not predominantly spherocytes. Osmotic fragility is usually normal. The DAT is negative. This doesn't fit the chronic presentation and specific morphology.
- (B): **Beta-Thalassemia:** This is a disorder of globin chain synthesis, leading to ineffective erythropoiesis and hemolysis. It typically presents with microcytic, hypochromic anemia (low MCV, MCH), target cells on smear, and often elevated HbA2 or HbF. Spherocytes are not the characteristic finding, and the MCV/MCH are usually low, not normal.
- (D): **Autoimmune Hemolytic Anemia (AIHA):** This involves antibody-mediated destruction of red cells. The DAT (Coombs test) is typically positive, detecting antibodies (IgG or IgM) on the RBC surface. While spherocytes can be seen in warm AIHA, the negative DAT in this patient rules out this diagnosis.
- (E): **Lead Poisoning (Plumbism):** Lead inhibits several enzymes in the heme synthesis pathway, including ferrochelatase and ALA dehydratase. This leads to anemia (often microcytic, but can be normocytic), basophilic stippling on the smear, and elevated levels of protoporphyrin IX in red cells. Spherocytes are not characteristic, and the clinical picture (jaundice, splenomegaly, specific smear findings) doesn't align.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Hereditary Spherocytosis (HS) | G6PD Deficiency | Beta-Thalassemia | Autoimmune Hemolysis (AIHA) | Lead Poisoning |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------- | :-------------- | :--------------- | :--------------------------- | :------------- |
| **Inheritance**         | Autosomal Dominant (most)     | X-linked        | Autosomal Recessive | N/A (usually)                | Environmental |
| **Anemia Type**         | Hemolytic                    | Hemolytic       | Hemolytic/Ineffective Erythropoiesis | Hemolytic                  | Microcytic/Normocytic |
| **MCV**                 | Normal                       | Normal          | Low              | Normal/High                  | Low/Normal    |
| **MCHC**                | High/Normal                   | Normal          | Normal           | Normal                       | Normal        |
| **Reticulocytes**       | High                         | High (during crisis) | High            | High                         | Low/Normal    |
| **Peripheral Smear**    | Spherocytes                   | Bite cells, Heinz bodies | Target cells, Microcytosis | Spherocytes (sometimes)     | Basophilic Stippling |
| **DAT**                 | Negative                     | Negative        | Negative         | Positive                     | Negative      |
| **Osmotic Fragility**   | Increased                    | Normal          | Normal           | Normal                       | Normal        |
| **Splenomegaly**        | Common                       | No              | Common           | Variable                     | No            |
| **Underlying Defect**   | Membrane protein (Spectrin, Ankyrin, Band 3, Protein 4.2) | G6PD enzyme    | Globin chain synthesis      | Autoantibodies            | Heme synthesis enzymes |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hereditary spherocytosis is characterized by defects in RBC membrane proteins leading to spherocytes, chronic extravascular hemolysis, jaundice, splenomegaly, and increased osmotic fragility. Differentiating it from other hemolytic anemias like G6PD deficiency (episodic, oxidative stress trigger, bite cells), thalassemia (microcytic, target cells), AIHA (positive DAT), and lead poisoning (basophilic stippling) relies on the specific clinical presentation, smear morphology, and laboratory findings, especially the negative DAT and increased osmotic fragility in HS.

<a id='question-050'></a>

### Question 050: RBC Pathology and Anemias: Focus 01-50 (spherical and have a bluish color (polychromasia)d)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of hereditary spherocytosis, specifically its pathophysiology involving defects in red blood cell membrane proteins leading to loss of membrane surface area, decreased deformability, and premature destruction in the spleen. It also tests the ability to interpret peripheral blood smear findings characteristic of this condition. (本题考察的是遗传性球形红细胞增多症的理解，特别是红细胞膜蛋白缺陷导致膜表面积减少、变形能力下降并在脾脏中过早破坏的病理生理机制。同时也考察理解该病变周围血涂片特征性发现的能力。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 6-year-old boy presents to the pediatrician with complaints of fatigue, jaundice, and dark urine. His mother reports that he has always been smaller than his peers and has had recurrent episodes of pallor. Physical examination reveals mild scleral icterus and splenomegaly. Laboratory studies show a hemoglobin of 8.5 g/dL (normal range: 11.5-15.5 g/dL), MCV of 80 fL (normal range: 80-100 fL), MCH of 25 pg (normal range: 27-33 pg), MCHC of 35 g/dL (normal range: 32-36 g/dL), and a reticulocyte count of 5% (normal range: 0.5-2.5%). A peripheral blood smear shows numerous small, round, densely staining red blood cells lacking central pallor, consistent with spherocytes. Osmotic fragility testing is markedly increased.

(A) Deficiency of glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD)
(B) Autoimmune hemolytic anemia
(C) Hereditary spherocytosis due to spectrin deficiency
(D) Sickle cell anemia
(E) Iron deficiency anemia

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 6-year-old boy presenting with symptoms suggestive of chronic hemolytic anemia (fatigue, jaundice, dark urine, pallor, smaller stature).
- **Physical Exam:** Scleral icterus (jaundice) and splenomegaly (site of RBC destruction).
- **Lab Findings:**
    - Anemia: Hemoglobin is low (8.5 g/dL).
    - Normocytic/Microcytic: MCV is normal (80 fL), but MCH is slightly low (25 pg), suggesting potentially microcytic or borderline normocytic anemia.
    - Increased MCHC: MCHC is high (35 g/dL), indicating increased concentration of hemoglobin within the RBCs, characteristic of spherocytes.
    - Increased Reticulocyte Count: Reticulocyte count is elevated (5%), indicating the bone marrow is responding to the hemolysis by increasing RBC production.
- **Peripheral Blood Smear:** Shows spherocytes (small, round, dense RBCs without central pallor).
- **Osmotic Fragility:** Markedly increased, indicating RBCs are less able to withstand hypotonic stress, a hallmark of spherocytes due to their reduced surface area-to-volume ratio.
- **Synthesis:** The combination of chronic hemolytic anemia, jaundice, splenomegaly, spherocytes on smear, increased MCHC, increased reticulocytes, and increased osmotic fragility strongly points towards hereditary spherocytosis. The underlying defect is typically in red blood cell membrane proteins, most commonly spectrin, ankyrin, band 3, or protein 4.2.

##### 2. Pathophysiological, Molecular & Pharmacological Deep-Dive (病理生理、分子机制与药理靶点深度剖析)
- **Hereditary Spherocytosis (HS):** An inherited disorder characterized by defects in RBC membrane proteins (e.g., spectrin, ankyrin, band 3, protein 4.2). These proteins are crucial for maintaining the RBC's biconcave shape, flexibility, and stability.
- **Mechanism:** Defects lead to loss of membrane surface area relative to cell volume. The RBC becomes spherical (spherocyte) instead of biconcave. Spherocytes are less deformable and are trapped and destroyed prematurely in the splenic sinusoids by splenic macrophages.
- **Consequences:** Chronic extravascular hemolysis, leading to anemia, jaundice (due to bilirubin from heme breakdown), splenomegaly (due to increased workload), and potentially gallstones (bilirubin stones).
- **Lab Findings:** Anemia (variable severity), increased MCHC (due to dehydration/loss of membrane), increased reticulocyte count (compensatory response), spherocytes on peripheral smear, increased osmotic fragility.
- **Genetic Basis:** Most commonly autosomal dominant inheritance (75%), involving mutations in genes encoding spectrin (alpha or beta), ankyrin, band 3, or protein 4.2. Autosomal recessive forms are less common and often more severe.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Deficiency of glucose-6-phosphate dehydrogenase (G6PD):** This is an X-linked enzyme deficiency causing episodic hemolytic anemia triggered by oxidative stress (e.g., certain drugs, infections, fava beans). While it causes hemolysis and jaundice, it typically presents with acute episodes, not chronic symptoms from infancy. The peripheral smear shows bite cells and Heinz bodies (denatured hemoglobin), not predominantly spherocytes. MCHC is usually normal. Osmotic fragility is normal.
- (B) **Autoimmune hemolytic anemia (AIHA):** This involves antibodies attacking RBCs, leading to hemolysis. It can be warm (IgG) or cold (IgM). While it causes anemia, jaundice, and splenomegaly, the peripheral smear typically shows spherocytes (due to partial phagocytosis by macrophages), but also polychromasia and possibly schistocytes. A direct Coombs test (DAT) would be positive, which is not mentioned here. The presentation is often more acute or subacute than the chronic history described.
- (D) **Sickle cell anemia:** An autosomal recessive disorder caused by a mutation in the beta-globin gene (HbS). It leads to vaso-occlusive crises, chronic hemolytic anemia, and organ damage. The peripheral smear shows characteristic sickle-shaped cells (drepanocytes), target cells, and Howell-Jolly bodies (due to splenic dysfunction). MCHC is usually normal. Osmotic fragility is normal. The clinical presentation is different, focusing on pain crises and organ damage.
- (E) **Iron deficiency anemia (IDA):** The most common type of anemia, caused by insufficient iron. It leads to microcytic, hypochromic anemia (low MCV, low MCH, low MCHC). Symptoms include fatigue and pallor. Jaundice and splenomegaly are not typical features. The peripheral smear shows microcytic, hypochromic RBCs, anisocytosis, and poikilocytosis. Reticulocyte count is low or normal. Osmotic fragility is normal. The lab findings (normal MCV, high MCHC) are inconsistent with IDA.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点横向对比与鉴别矩阵):

| Feature                 | Hereditary Spherocytosis | G6PD Deficiency | AIHA                 | Sickle Cell Anemia | Iron Deficiency Anemia |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------- | :-------------- | :------------------- | :---------------- | :--------------------- |
| **Inheritance**         | AD (most common)        | X-linked        | Acquired             | AR                | Acquired/Nutritional   |
| **Hemolysis Type**      | Extravascular (spleen)   | Intravascular/Extravascular | Extravascular/Intravascular | Extravascular/Intravascular | N/A                   |
| **Typical RBC Morphology** | Spherocytes              | Bite cells, Heinz bodies | Spherocytes, Polychromasia | Sickle cells, Target cells | Microcytic, Hypochromic |
| **MCHC**                | Increased                | Normal          | Normal/Increased     | Normal            | Decreased              |
| **Reticulocytes**       | Increased                | Increased (during crisis) | Increased           | Increased         | Decreased/Normal       |
| **Osmotic Fragility**   | Increased                | Normal          | Normal/Increased     | Normal            | Normal                 |
| **Splenomegaly**        | Common                   | Absent          | Common               | Variable (autosplenectomy) | Absent                 |
| **Jaundice**            | Common                   | Common (during crisis) | Common               | Common            | Absent                 |
| **Trigger**             | Genetic defect           | Oxidative stress | Autoantibodies       | Hypoxia           | Iron deficiency        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Hereditary spherocytosis is characterized by chronic extravascular hemolysis due to RBC membrane defects, leading to spherocytes, increased MCHC, increased osmotic fragility, jaundice, and splenomegaly. Key differentials include other hemolytic anemias like G6PD deficiency (episodic, bite cells), AIHA (positive Coombs test), and sickle cell anemia (sickle cells). Iron deficiency anemia is microcytic/hypochromic and lacks hemolysis features.
